I guess I came in because... I just can't shake this feeling that I've failed. Failed as a father, and really just as a useful person in general. I don't know. I was actually thinking about that on the drive over here. The traffic on the highway was backed up for miles because of some construction. They've been working on that same stretch of road for, what, three years now? It feels like they just move the orange cones back and forth without actually fixing anything. I used to do project management years ago, and if we ran a site like that... well, anyway. 

Sitting in that traffic, I just had these same thoughts looping in my head. About how useless I am. They just keep looping, over and over, until I start feeling like... like I really don't deserve much of a future.

I don't know, it's... I guess I really haven't been enjoying much of anything lately. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway setup in the basement. It was something my daughter and I used to tinker with when she was younger. I've got this whole O-gauge layout, you know? The classic Lionel trains. I was trying to wire up this new crossing signal before she left for school, but... now the trains just sit there half-built on the table gathering dust. 

We used to watch the Cubs games together, too. She's a huge fan. We went to Wrigley a few years back, sat right behind the dugout. The seats were so cramped, though, and the guy next to me kept spilling his beer every time someone got a hit. You'd think for what they charge for tickets these days, they'd give you a little more legroom. But anyway... now, even just turning on a game on the TV feels completely pointless. I just stare at the screen and I can't bring myself to care about the score or who's pitching or any of it. It's just... hollow.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... yeah. I have. It's not like I have a plan or anything, but I've been having these thoughts that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up in the morning. Just... go to sleep and that's it. It actually really scared me the other night, because I caught myself going through all my old bank statements and life insurance policies, making sure all the beneficiaries were updated, like I was getting things ready for someone else to take over. 

I had to pull all those files out of this old filing cabinet I keep in the spare room. It's one of those heavy metal ones, you know? Must weigh a hundred pounds empty. I bought it at a government surplus auction back in the nineties. They used to hold them at the old armory downtown, before they tore it down to build that new shopping center. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.

It was something my daughter and I used to tinker with when she was younger. I've got this whole O-gauge layout, you know? The classic Lionel trains. I was trying to wire up this new crossing signal before she left for school, but... now the trains just sit there half-built on the table gathering dust. \n\nWe used to watch the Cubs games together, too. She's a huge fan. We went to Wrigley a few years back, sat right behind the dugout. The seats were so cramped, though, and the guy next to me kept spilling his beer every time someone got a hit. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling slowed down or lethargic lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know... I mean, I don't think I'm restless. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses most of the time. Everything takes so much effort. Just getting up from the couch feels like a monumental task. I was trying to get up to check the mail yesterday, and I just sat there staring at the front door for what felt like an hour. Our mail carrier, he's this older guy, always wears those little blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how he does it. He's been delivering our mail for probably fifteen years. He always used to leave a milk bone for the neighbor's golden retriever, but they moved away last spring. Sold the house to some young couple from the city who immediately painted the beautiful original brick exterior this awful dark gray color. It completely ruins the character of the street. And they put up one of those modern, horizontal slat fences that just looks like a giant pallet. The neighborhood association sent them a letter about it, but apparently, the bylaws are too vague to actually enforce anything. It caused a huge argument at the last HOA meeting. The president of the board, Susan, got so red in the face I thought she was going to have a stroke right there in the community center. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, I don't feel restless. Just... heavy. Like I can't get going.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying... I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... But, um, yeah, everything just feels really slow and heavy right now.

No, I... I can't really think of any time lately where I felt better. It's just been this same heavy feeling every day. Actually, I was looking at the calendar on the wall yesterday, trying to figure out what day it was. It's one of those scenic calendars, you know? You'd think for what they charge for tickets these days, they'd give you a little more legroom. But anyway... now, even just turning on a game on the TV feels completely pointless. I just stare at the screen and I can't bring myself to care about the score or who's pitching or any of it. It's just... hollow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something like it? I'm sorry, my head is just... it's like a fog in there. But yeah, like I was saying, I just don't care about the trains or the baseball games anymore. Or anything, really. It's funny, speaking of the baseball games, I was trying to cancel my cable package the other day because what's the point of paying for the sports channels if I'm not watching them, right? And the customer service rep... I swear, they train those people to just read from a script and ignore everything you say. I was on the phone for forty-five minutes. Forty-five minutes! Just listening to that awful hold music. It sounded like it was recorded underwater. And then when I finally got a real person, they tried to upsell me on a faster internet package. I told them, I don't need faster internet, I just want to drop the sports channels. But they just kept pushing... I don't even remember if I actually got it canceled in the end. I just hung up. What were we talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, I just don't have the energy for the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I... yeah. I have. It's not like I have a plan or anything, but I've been having these thoughts that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up in the morning. Just... go to sleep and that's it. It actually really scared me the other night, because I caught myself going through all my old bank statements and life insurance policies, making sure all the beneficiaries were updated, like I was getting things ready for someone else to take over. \n\nI had to pull all those files out of this old filing cabinet I keep in the spare room. It's one of those heavy metal ones, you know? Must weigh a hundred pounds empty. I bought it at a government surplus auction back in the nineties. They used to hold them at the old armory downtown, before they tore it down to build that new shopping center. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling slowed down or lethargic lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I don't think I'm restless. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses most of the time. Everything takes so much effort. Just getting up from the couch feels like a monumental task. I was trying to get up to check the mail yesterday, and I just sat there staring at the front door for what felt like an hour. Our mail carrier, he's this older guy, always wears those little blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how he does it. He's been delivering our mail for probably fifteen years. He always used to leave a milk bone for the neighbor's golden retriever, but they moved away last spring. Sold the house to some young couple from the city who immediately painted the beautiful original brick exterior this awful dark gray color. It completely ruins the character of the street. And they put up one of those modern, horizontal slat fences that just looks like a giant pallet. The neighborhood association sent them a letter about it, but apparently, the bylaws are too vague to actually enforce anything. It caused a huge argument at the last HOA meeting. The president of the board, Susan, got so red in the face I thought she was going to have a stroke right there in the community center. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, I don't feel restless. Just... heavy. Like I can't get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... But, um, yeah, everything just feels really slow and heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't really think of any time lately where I felt better. It's just been this same heavy feeling every day. Actually, I was looking at the calendar on the wall yesterday, trying to figure out what day it was. It's one of those scenic calendars, you know? The one for this month has a picture of a lighthouse on the coast of Maine. I went to Maine once, years ago, with my ex-wife. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that served blueberry pancakes every morning. The owner was this lovely woman who knitted these intricate wool sweaters. She had a whole shop attached to the inn. I bought a dark blue one, but I accidentally shrank it in the wash a few years later. It's a shame, really, it was so warm. But anyway, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a break in it. It's just been the same.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's been about a month, I think. Yeah, right around the time my daughter left for college out of state. She went out to Oregon, you know. It's so far away. We drove her out there, took us four days. The drive through South Dakota was beautiful, though. We stopped at the Badlands, and it just looks like another planet. The rock formations are incredible, all these layers of pink and gray and tan. We stayed at this little motel near the park entrance that had this neon sign that kept buzzing and flickering all night. It drove my wife crazy, but I kind of liked it. Reminded me of this diner we used to go to when I was in grad school. They had the best cherry pie, always served it warm with a scoop of vanilla ice cream melting over the top. The waitress there, Betty, she always called everyone "hon" and remembered exactly how you took your coffee. I wonder if that place is still open. A lot of those old diners are closing down now, getting replaced by these sterile coffee shops where a cup of drip coffee costs five dollars. It's ridiculous. But... yeah, about a month. Ever since she left, I just haven't been able to shake this heavy feeling.

Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling slowed down or lethargic lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I don't think I'm restless. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses most of the time. Everything takes so much effort. Just getting up from the couch feels like a monumental task. I was trying to get up to check the mail yesterday, and I just sat there staring at the front door for what felt like an hour. Our mail carrier, he's this older guy, always wears those little blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how he does it. He's been delivering our mail for probably fifteen years. He always used to leave a milk bone for the neighbor's golden retriever, but they moved away last spring. Sold the house to some young couple from the city who immediately painted the beautiful original brick exterior this awful dark gray color. It completely ruins the character of the street. And they put up one of those modern, horizontal slat fences that just looks like a giant pallet. The neighborhood association sent them a letter about it, but apparently, the bylaws are too vague to actually enforce anything. It caused a huge argument at the last HOA meeting. The president of the board, Susan, got so red in the face I thought she was going to have a stroke right there in the community center. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, I don't feel restless. Just... heavy. Like I can't get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... But, um, yeah, everything just feels really slow and heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't really think of any time lately where I felt better. It's just been this same heavy feeling every day. Actually, I was looking at the calendar on the wall yesterday, trying to figure out what day it was. It's one of those scenic calendars, you know? The one for this month has a picture of a lighthouse on the coast of Maine. I went to Maine once, years ago, with my ex-wife. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that served blueberry pancakes every morning. The owner was this lovely woman who knitted these intricate wool sweaters. She had a whole shop attached to the inn. I bought a dark blue one, but I accidentally shrank it in the wash a few years later. It's a shame, really, it was so warm. But anyway, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a break in it. It's just been the same.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy and slow, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: It's been about a month, I think. Yeah, right around the time my daughter left for college out of state. She went out to Oregon, you know. It's so far away. We drove her out there, took us four days. The drive through South Dakota was beautiful, though. We stopped at the Badlands, and it just looks like another planet. The rock formations are incredible, all these layers of pink and gray and tan. We stayed at this little motel near the park entrance that had this neon sign that kept buzzing and flickering all night. It drove my wife crazy, but I kind of liked it. Reminded me of this diner we used to go to when I was in grad school. They had the best cherry pie, always served it warm with a scoop of vanilla ice cream melting over the top. The waitress there, Betty, she always called everyone \"hon\" and remembered exactly how you took your coffee. I wonder if that place is still open. A lot of those old diners are closing down now, getting replaced by these sterile coffee shops where a cup of drip coffee costs five dollars. It's ridiculous. But... yeah, about a month. Ever since she left, I just haven't been able to shake this heavy feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Ever since she left, I just haven't been able to shake this heavy feeling.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt this way before, like you're a failure and don't enjoy things, at any other point in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, everyone has bad days, but nothing like this. It's just this constant, heavy cloud. It reminds me of the weather we had back in '98, during that awful El Niño winter. It just rained and rained for weeks. The ground was so saturated that my neighbor's retaining wall completely collapsed into his driveway. He had this beautiful vintage Mustang parked there, a '67, cherry red with white racing stripes. It was completely crushed by the mud and rocks. He was devastated, of course. He used to spend every Saturday morning out there polishing it, listening to old Motown records on this little portable radio he had. I always liked the Supremes, myself. Diana Ross had such a unique voice. I actually saw her in concert once, back when I was still in grad school. My roommate managed to get front-row tickets because his uncle worked at the venue. We had to take the subway across the city, and the train broke down for an hour... but anyway, no, to answer your question, I've never felt this much like a failure before. This is all new.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because... I just can't shake this feeling that I've failed. Failed as a father, and really just as a useful person in general. I don't know. I was actually thinking about that on the drive over here. The traffic on the highway was backed up for miles because of some construction. They've been working on that same stretch of road for, what, three years now? It feels like they just move the orange cones back and forth without actually fixing anything. I used to do project management years ago, and if we ran a site like that... well, anyway. \n\nSitting in that traffic, I just had these same thoughts looping in my head. About how useless I am. They just keep looping, over and over, until I start feeling like... like I really don't deserve much of a future.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about being a failure?\n\nPatient: It's been... I guess about a month now. Yeah, ever since my daughter left for college. She went out of state, so we had to drive her down there, and... I don't know, the whole trip was just a mess. The AC in my car gave out halfway there. I kept meaning to take it to the shop before we left, but I just never got around to it. So we drove for hours with the windows down, and the wind noise on the interstate was just deafening. \n\nAnd then we get to the campus, and her dorm is just this tiny little box. You pay all this out-of-state tuition, and they put her in a room where you can't even open the door without hitting the bed. I tried to help her set up some shelves she bought, but I realized I left my good tool bag sitting on the workbench back home. Just completely forgot it. So I had to use this cheap little screwdriver from a campus convenience store...\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you normally like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don't know, it's... I guess I really haven't been enjoying much of anything lately. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway setup in the basement. It was something my daughter and I used to tinker with when she was younger. I've got this whole O-gauge layout, you know? The classic Lionel trains. I was trying to wire up this new crossing signal before she left for school, but... now the trains just sit there half-built on the table gathering dust. \n\nWe used to watch the Cubs games together, too. She's a huge fan. We went to Wrigley a few years back, sat right behind the dugout. The seats were so cramped, though, and the guy next to me kept spilling his beer every time someone got a hit. You'd think for what they charge for tickets these days, they'd give you a little more legroom. But anyway... now, even just turning on a game on the TV feels completely pointless. I just stare at the screen and I can't bring myself to care about the score or who's pitching or any of it. It's just... hollow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something like it? I'm sorry, my head is just... it's like a fog in there. But yeah, like I was saying, I just don't care about the trains or the baseball games anymore. Or anything, really. It's funny, speaking of the baseball games, I was trying to cancel my cable package the other day because what's the point of paying for the sports channels if I'm not watching them, right? And the customer service rep... I swear, they train those people to just read from a script and ignore everything you say. I was on the phone for forty-five minutes. Forty-five minutes! Just listening to that awful hold music. It sounded like it was recorded underwater. And then when I finally got a real person, they tried to upsell me on a faster internet package. I told them, I don't need faster internet, I just want to drop the sports channels. But they just kept pushing... I don't even remember if I actually got it canceled in the end. I just hung up. What were we talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, I just don't have the energy for the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I... yeah. I have. It's not like I have a plan or anything, but I've been having these thoughts that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up in the morning. Just... go to sleep and that's it. It actually really scared me the other night, because I caught myself going through all my old bank statements and life insurance policies, making sure all the beneficiaries were updated, like I was getting things ready for someone else to take over. \n\nI had to pull all those files out of this old filing cabinet I keep in the spare room. It's one of those heavy metal ones, you know? Must weigh a hundred pounds empty. I bought it at a government surplus auction back in the nineties. They used to hold them at the old armory downtown, before they tore it down to build that new shopping center. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling slowed down or lethargic lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I don't think I'm restless. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses most of the time. Everything takes so much effort. Just getting up from the couch feels like a monumental task. I was trying to get up to check the mail yesterday, and I just sat there staring at the front door for what felt like an hour. Our mail carrier, he's this older guy, always wears those little blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how he does it. He's been delivering our mail for probably fifteen years. He always used to leave a milk bone for the neighbor's golden retriever, but they moved away last spring. Sold the house to some young couple from the city who immediately painted the beautiful original brick exterior this awful dark gray color. It completely ruins the character of the street. And they put up one of those modern, horizontal slat fences that just looks like a giant pallet. The neighborhood association sent them a letter about it, but apparently, the bylaws are too vague to actually enforce anything. It caused a huge argument at the last HOA meeting. The president of the board, Susan, got so red in the face I thought she was going to have a stroke right there in the community center. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, I don't feel restless. Just... heavy. Like I can't get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... But, um, yeah, everything just feels really slow and heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't really think of any time lately where I felt better. It's just been this same heavy feeling every day. Actually, I was looking at the calendar on the wall yesterday, trying to figure out what day it was. It's one of those scenic calendars, you know? The one for this month has a picture of a lighthouse on the coast of Maine. I went to Maine once, years ago, with my ex-wife. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that served blueberry pancakes every morning. The owner was this lovely woman who knitted these intricate wool sweaters. She had a whole shop attached to the inn. I bought a dark blue one, but I accidentally shrank it in the wash a few years later. It's a shame, really, it was so warm. But anyway, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a break in it. It's just been the same.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy and slow, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: It's been about a month, I think. Yeah, right around the time my daughter left for college out of state. She went out to Oregon, you know. It's so far away. We drove her out there, took us four days. The drive through South Dakota was beautiful, though. We stopped at the Badlands, and it just looks like another planet. The rock formations are incredible, all these layers of pink and gray and tan. We stayed at this little motel near the park entrance that had this neon sign that kept buzzing and flickering all night. It drove my wife crazy, but I kind of liked it. Reminded me of this diner we used to go to when I was in grad school. They had the best cherry pie, always served it warm with a scoop of vanilla ice cream melting over the top. The waitress there, Betty, she always called everyone \"hon\" and remembered exactly how you took your coffee. I wonder if that place is still open. A lot of those old diners are closing down now, getting replaced by these sterile coffee shops where a cup of drip coffee costs five dollars. It's ridiculous. But... yeah, about a month. Ever since she left, I just haven't been able to shake this heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're a failure and don't enjoy things, at any other point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, everyone has bad days, but nothing like this. It's just this constant, heavy cloud. It reminds me of the weather we had back in '98, during that awful El Niño winter. It just rained and rained for weeks. The ground was so saturated that my neighbor's retaining wall completely collapsed into his driveway. He had this beautiful vintage Mustang parked there, a '67, cherry red with white racing stripes. It was completely crushed by the mud and rocks. He was devastated, of course. He used to spend every Saturday morning out there polishing it, listening to old Motown records on this little portable radio he had. I always liked the Supremes, myself. Diana Ross had such a unique voice. I actually saw her in concert once, back when I was still in grad school. My roommate managed to get front-row tickets because his uncle worked at the venue. We had to take the subway across the city, and the train broke down for an hour... but anyway, no, to answer your question, I've never felt this much like a failure before. This is all new.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not... not before she left. I mean, I was always pretty steady. I worked hard, provided for the family. I actually worked in logistics for twenty-five years, coordinating freight trains across the Midwest. It's a high-pressure job, you know, making sure everything runs on time. The train yards are fascinating places, though. All those massive steel cars clanging together, the smell of diesel and hot metal. I used to have this supervisor, Frank, who had worked on the railroads since the seventies. He had these incredible stories about the old cabooses they used to use before everything went electronic. He collected vintage model trains, the Lionel ones from the fifties. He had a whole setup in his basement with tiny little towns and mountains made of papier-mâché. He even painted little people waiting at the stations. I always thought it would be nice to have a hobby like that, something to focus on. But I just never had the time with work and raising my daughter. She used to love trains when she was a toddler, actually. We bought her this wooden set, and she'd spend hours pushing the little magnetic cars around the track... but no, before she went to Oregon, I never felt depressed like this. Never.

It's one of those heavy metal ones, you know? Must weigh a hundred pounds empty. I bought it at a government surplus auction back in the nineties. They used to hold them at the old armory downtown, before they tore it down to build that new shopping center. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... The one for this month has a picture of a lighthouse on the coast of Maine. I went to Maine once, years ago, with my ex-wife. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that served blueberry pancakes every morning. The owner was this lovely woman who knitted these intricate wool sweaters. She had a whole shop attached to the inn. I bought a dark blue one, but I accidentally shrank it in the wash a few years later. It's a shame, really, it was so warm. But anyway, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a break in it. It's just been the same.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy and slow, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: It's been about a month, I think. Yeah, right around the time my daughter left for college out of state. She went out to Oregon, you know. It's so far away. We drove her out there, took us four days. The drive through South Dakota was beautiful, though. We stopped at the Badlands, and it just looks like another planet. The rock formations are incredible, all these layers of pink and gray and tan. We stayed at this little motel near the park entrance that had this neon sign that kept buzzing and flickering all night. It drove my wife crazy, but I kind of liked it. Reminded me of this diner we used to go to when I was in grad school. They had the best cherry pie, always served it warm with a scoop of vanilla ice cream melting over the top. The waitress there, Betty, she always called everyone \"hon\" and remembered exactly how you took your coffee. I wonder if that place is still open. A lot of those old diners are closing down now, getting replaced by these sterile coffee shops where a cup of drip coffee costs five dollars. It's ridiculous. But... yeah, about a month. It was completely crushed by the mud and rocks. He was devastated, of course. It's a high-pressure job, you know, making sure everything runs on time. The train yards are fascinating places, though. All those massive steel cars clanging together, the smell of diesel and hot metal. I used to have this supervisor, Frank, who had worked on the railroads since the seventies. He had these incredible stories about the old cabooses they used to use before everything went electronic. He collected vintage model trains, the Lionel ones from the fifties. He had a whole setup in his basement with tiny little towns and mountains made of papier-mâché. He even painted little people waiting at the stations. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I've never been diagnosed with it. I've hardly ever even been to a doctor, to be honest. I mean, besides the usual checkups and that one time I broke my ankle playing softball in my thirties. That was a mess. I slid into second base, and the ground was just rock hard because we hadn't had rain in weeks. The dust kicked up everywhere, and I heard this loud pop. The guy playing shortstop, Dave, he was a paramedic, actually. Thank god he was there. He knew exactly what to do, kept me calm while we waited for the ambulance. The hospital food was terrible, though. I remember they gave me this jello that was supposed to be cherry flavored, but it just tasted like plastic. My wife ended up sneaking in a meatball sub from this Italian deli down the street from the hospital. The guy who runs it, Tony, he makes his own mozzarella every morning. You can really taste the difference, you know? The cheese is just so soft and fresh. He imports the olive oil straight from his cousin's farm in Sicily. He showed me pictures of the olive groves once, these ancient, twisted trees that have been there for hundreds of years... but anyway, no, never diagnosed with depression or anything like that. Just the broken ankle.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because... I just can't shake this feeling that I've failed. Failed as a father, and really just as a useful person in general. I don't know. I was actually thinking about that on the drive over here. The traffic on the highway was backed up for miles because of some construction. They've been working on that same stretch of road for, what, three years now? It feels like they just move the orange cones back and forth without actually fixing anything. I used to do project management years ago, and if we ran a site like that... well, anyway. \n\nSitting in that traffic, I just had these same thoughts looping in my head. About how useless I am. They just keep looping, over and over, until I start feeling like... like I really don't deserve much of a future.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about being a failure?\n\nPatient: It's been... I guess about a month now. Yeah, ever since my daughter left for college. She went out of state, so we had to drive her down there, and... I don't know, the whole trip was just a mess. The AC in my car gave out halfway there. I kept meaning to take it to the shop before we left, but I just never got around to it. So we drove for hours with the windows down, and the wind noise on the interstate was just deafening. \n\nAnd then we get to the campus, and her dorm is just this tiny little box. You pay all this out-of-state tuition, and they put her in a room where you can't even open the door without hitting the bed. I tried to help her set up some shelves she bought, but I realized I left my good tool bag sitting on the workbench back home. Just completely forgot it. So I had to use this cheap little screwdriver from a campus convenience store...\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you normally like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don't know, it's... I guess I really haven't been enjoying much of anything lately. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway setup in the basement. It was something my daughter and I used to tinker with when she was younger. I've got this whole O-gauge layout, you know? The classic Lionel trains. I was trying to wire up this new crossing signal before she left for school, but... now the trains just sit there half-built on the table gathering dust. \n\nWe used to watch the Cubs games together, too. She's a huge fan. We went to Wrigley a few years back, sat right behind the dugout. The seats were so cramped, though, and the guy next to me kept spilling his beer every time someone got a hit. You'd think for what they charge for tickets these days, they'd give you a little more legroom. But anyway... now, even just turning on a game on the TV feels completely pointless. I just stare at the screen and I can't bring myself to care about the score or who's pitching or any of it. It's just... hollow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something like it? I'm sorry, my head is just... it's like a fog in there. But yeah, like I was saying, I just don't care about the trains or the baseball games anymore. Or anything, really. It's funny, speaking of the baseball games, I was trying to cancel my cable package the other day because what's the point of paying for the sports channels if I'm not watching them, right? And the customer service rep... I swear, they train those people to just read from a script and ignore everything you say. I was on the phone for forty-five minutes. Forty-five minutes! Just listening to that awful hold music. It sounded like it was recorded underwater. And then when I finally got a real person, they tried to upsell me on a faster internet package. I told them, I don't need faster internet, I just want to drop the sports channels. But they just kept pushing... I don't even remember if I actually got it canceled in the end. I just hung up. What were we talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, I just don't have the energy for the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I... yeah. I have. It's not like I have a plan or anything, but I've been having these thoughts that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up in the morning. Just... go to sleep and that's it. It actually really scared me the other night, because I caught myself going through all my old bank statements and life insurance policies, making sure all the beneficiaries were updated, like I was getting things ready for someone else to take over. \n\nI had to pull all those files out of this old filing cabinet I keep in the spare room. It's one of those heavy metal ones, you know? Must weigh a hundred pounds empty. I bought it at a government surplus auction back in the nineties. They used to hold them at the old armory downtown, before they tore it down to build that new shopping center. The one with the massive parking garage where the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your car door. I parked there once to go to the hardware store, and some idiot in a huge truck parked right on the line next to me. I had to practically climb over my center console to get into the driver's seat. I don't understand why people buy those massive trucks if they don't even use them for work. They just take up space... But yeah, the thoughts. They're there.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, most days I just don't even bother making a real meal anymore. I've had to tighten my belt a notch or two because my pants are getting loose. I just... I completely forget to eat until it's really late, and even then, I only manage a piece of toast or a bowl of cereal. I was eating some of that honey nut cereal the other night, the one with the little bee on the box. You know, I read an article the other day about how the bee populations are collapsing everywhere. Something about pesticides they use on the big commercial farms. It's terrible, really. If the bees go, we lose so much of our agriculture. I tried planting a pollinator garden in the backyard a couple of years ago to help out. Bought all these native wildflowers, milkweed, coneflowers, the whole bit. Spent a fortune on soil amendments, too. The guy at the garden center swore by this organic compost, said it was practically magic. But then the deer came through and ate everything down to the stems. They are just a nuisance in my neighborhood. They have no fear of people anymore. They'll just stand right in the middle of the road and stare at your headlights. I almost hit one last winter coming back from the pharmacy. The roads were icy, and... well, anyway. But yeah, to answer your question, I have lost some weight because I'm just not eating much besides cereal and toast.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling slowed down or lethargic lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I don't think I'm restless. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses most of the time. Everything takes so much effort. Just getting up from the couch feels like a monumental task. I was trying to get up to check the mail yesterday, and I just sat there staring at the front door for what felt like an hour. Our mail carrier, he's this older guy, always wears those little blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how he does it. He's been delivering our mail for probably fifteen years. He always used to leave a milk bone for the neighbor's golden retriever, but they moved away last spring. Sold the house to some young couple from the city who immediately painted the beautiful original brick exterior this awful dark gray color. It completely ruins the character of the street. And they put up one of those modern, horizontal slat fences that just looks like a giant pallet. The neighborhood association sent them a letter about it, but apparently, the bylaws are too vague to actually enforce anything. It caused a huge argument at the last HOA meeting. The president of the board, Susan, got so red in the face I thought she was going to have a stroke right there in the community center. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, I don't feel restless. Just... heavy. Like I can't get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I feel like I'm moving through molasses. It's like my arms and legs are made of lead. So yeah, I guess I'm moving a lot slower. It's funny, my grandfather used to say that about getting older, that you just slow down. He lived to be ninety-two, you know. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his hometown. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. I still have it in my garage somewhere, behind all the boxes of old books I keep meaning to donate. I should really take those to the library. They have a book sale every summer to raise money for the children's reading program. My kids used to go to that when they were little. They loved the puppet shows they put on. They had this one puppet, a big green dragon, that always made my daughter laugh so hard she'd get the hiccups. She'd try to hold her breath to get rid of them, turning completely red in the face... But, um, yeah, everything just feels really slow and heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't really think of any time lately where I felt better. It's just been this same heavy feeling every day. Actually, I was looking at the calendar on the wall yesterday, trying to figure out what day it was. It's one of those scenic calendars, you know? The one for this month has a picture of a lighthouse on the coast of Maine. I went to Maine once, years ago, with my ex-wife. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that served blueberry pancakes every morning. The owner was this lovely woman who knitted these intricate wool sweaters. She had a whole shop attached to the inn. I bought a dark blue one, but I accidentally shrank it in the wash a few years later. It's a shame, really, it was so warm. But anyway, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a break in it. It's just been the same.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy and slow, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: It's been about a month, I think. Yeah, right around the time my daughter left for college out of state. She went out to Oregon, you know. It's so far away. We drove her out there, took us four days. The drive through South Dakota was beautiful, though. We stopped at the Badlands, and it just looks like another planet. The rock formations are incredible, all these layers of pink and gray and tan. We stayed at this little motel near the park entrance that had this neon sign that kept buzzing and flickering all night. It drove my wife crazy, but I kind of liked it. Reminded me of this diner we used to go to when I was in grad school. They had the best cherry pie, always served it warm with a scoop of vanilla ice cream melting over the top. The waitress there, Betty, she always called everyone \"hon\" and remembered exactly how you took your coffee. I wonder if that place is still open. A lot of those old diners are closing down now, getting replaced by these sterile coffee shops where a cup of drip coffee costs five dollars. It's ridiculous. But... yeah, about a month. Ever since she left, I just haven't been able to shake this heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're a failure and don't enjoy things, at any other point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, everyone has bad days, but nothing like this. It's just this constant, heavy cloud. It reminds me of the weather we had back in '98, during that awful El Niño winter. It just rained and rained for weeks. The ground was so saturated that my neighbor's retaining wall completely collapsed into his driveway. He had this beautiful vintage Mustang parked there, a '67, cherry red with white racing stripes. It was completely crushed by the mud and rocks. He was devastated, of course. He used to spend every Saturday morning out there polishing it, listening to old Motown records on this little portable radio he had. I always liked the Supremes, myself. Diana Ross had such a unique voice. I actually saw her in concert once, back when I was still in grad school. My roommate managed to get front-row tickets because his uncle worked at the venue. We had to take the subway across the city, and the train broke down for an hour... but anyway, no, to answer your question, I've never felt this much like a failure before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're depressed, at any other point in your life, before your daughter left for college?\n\nPatient: No, not... not before she left. I mean, I was always pretty steady. I worked hard, provided for the family. I actually worked in logistics for twenty-five years, coordinating freight trains across the Midwest. It's a high-pressure job, you know, making sure everything runs on time. The train yards are fascinating places, though. All those massive steel cars clanging together, the smell of diesel and hot metal. I used to have this supervisor, Frank, who had worked on the railroads since the seventies. He had these incredible stories about the old cabooses they used to use before everything went electronic. He collected vintage model trains, the Lionel ones from the fifties. He had a whole setup in his basement with tiny little towns and mountains made of papier-mâché. He even painted little people waiting at the stations. I always thought it would be nice to have a hobby like that, something to focus on. But I just never had the time with work and raising my daughter. She used to love trains when she was a toddler, actually. We bought her this wooden set, and she'd spend hours pushing the little magnetic cars around the track... but no, before she went to Oregon, I never felt depressed like this. Never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been diagnosed with it. I've hardly ever even been to a doctor, to be honest. I mean, besides the usual checkups and that one time I broke my ankle playing softball in my thirties. That was a mess. I slid into second base, and the ground was just rock hard because we hadn't had rain in weeks. The dust kicked up everywhere, and I heard this loud pop. The guy playing shortstop, Dave, he was a paramedic, actually. Thank god he was there. He knew exactly what to do, kept me calm while we waited for the ambulance. The hospital food was terrible, though. I remember they gave me this jello that was supposed to be cherry flavored, but it just tasted like plastic. My wife ended up sneaking in a meatball sub from this Italian deli down the street from the hospital. The guy who runs it, Tony, he makes his own mozzarella every morning. You can really taste the difference, you know? The cheese is just so soft and fresh. He imports the olive oil straight from his cousin's farm in Sicily. He showed me pictures of the olive groves once, these ancient, twisted trees that have been there for hundreds of years... but anyway, no, never diagnosed with depression or anything like that. Just the broken ankle.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

No... I don't think I ever did. Not like this. Everything was pretty normal before we packed her up. We rented one of those U-Haul trailers, the small ones, to hitch to the back of the Subaru. He had this old wooden toolbox he made himself, with all these little compartments for different sized nails and screws. It smelled like sawdust and oil. We bought her this wooden set, and she'd spend hours pushing the little magnetic cars around the track... but no, before she went to Oregon, I never felt depressed like this. Never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been diagnosed with it. I've hardly ever even been to a doctor, to be honest. I mean, besides the usual checkups and that one time I broke my ankle playing softball in my thirties. That was a mess. I slid into second base, and the ground was just rock hard because we hadn't had rain in weeks. The dust kicked up everywhere, and I heard this loud pop. The guy playing shortstop, Dave, he was a paramedic, actually. Thank god he was there. He knew exactly what to do, kept me calm while we waited for the ambulance. The hospital food was terrible, though. I remember they gave me this jello that was supposed to be cherry flavored, but it just tasted like plastic. My wife ended up sneaking in a meatball sub from this Italian deli down the street from the hospital. The guy who runs it, Tony, he makes his own mozzarella every morning. You can really taste the difference, you know? The cheese is just so soft and fresh. He imports the olive oil straight from his cousin's farm in Sicily. He showed me pictures of the olive groves once, these ancient, twisted trees that have been there for hundreds of years... but anyway, no, never diagnosed with depression or anything like that. Just the broken ankle.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling this low or depressed before your daughter left for college?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think I ever did. Not like this. Everything was pretty normal before we packed her up. We rented one of those U-Haul trailers, the small ones, to hitch to the back of the Subaru. I had to go to three different places just to get the right size hitch ball installed. The guy at the auto parts store, he was this young kid, maybe twenty, studying engineering at the state college. He was telling me all about his thermodynamics class. I took thermodynamics back in my grad program, actually. It was a brutal class. Professor Vance, he was this incredibly strict guy who always wore these thick tweed jackets with the leather elbow patches. Real old school. He used to smoke a pipe right there in the lecture hall. You could never get away with that now, obviously. The whole room always smelled like cherry tobacco. It's a nice smell, actually, kind of sweet and comforting. My grandfather smoked a pipe too, mostly out on his back porch in the summers. He had this old wooden rocking chair that squeaked every time he leaned back... but, um... what was I saying? Right, feeling low. No, I never felt this bad before she moved out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had an episode like this before, where you felt depressed for an extended period of time, that then got better on its own or with treatment?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I... I really don't think so. I've never had anything like this happen before, where I just couldn't shake it off. I mean, I've had bad days, sure. Everyone has bad days. Like when the basement flooded back in '08. That was a nightmare. The sump pump just gave out in the middle of this massive thunderstorm. We had three inches of standing water down there. It ruined all these old photo albums my wife had stored in cardboard boxes. You'd think we would have known better than to use cardboard, right? But you just don't think about it until it happens. We had to hire this restoration company to come in with these giant industrial fans. They sounded like jet engines running in the house for three days straight. The guy who set them up, he was telling me he used to work on actual jet engines in the Air Force. He was stationed in Germany for a few years in the nineties. He said the beer over there was incredible, completely different from the stuff we have here. He actually started brewing his own beer in his garage after he got back. He gave me a bottle of this stout he made, it was really dark and thick, almost like motor oil... but, uh, no. To answer your question, no. I've never been stuck in a rut like this before. It's completely new.
